← 02.1 Graph · G1 call stack MAX / CONTEXTS / SHARED SERVICES · SOURCE

MAXContext creation, MLIR and shared services

Copied answers and a deeper inventory of the native MAX context.

QnA 1 · MAX Context — where is this created in G1?

G1 triggers MAX Context initialization near the end of Graph.__init__(), at graph.py:909:

self._kernel_library = kernel_library or KernelLibrary()

In 02.1 Graph · G1, find the branch:

KernelLibrary(); _import_kernels(resolved extension paths)

The creation path beneath it is:

Graph.__init__()
└─ KernelLibrary()
   └─ KernelLibrary.__init__()
      ├─ context = default_mlir_context() → MLIR CTX0
      └─ _graph._init_and_register_max_context(CTX0)
         └─ M::Init::getOrCreateContext("max-graph", options)
            └─ if no global MAX Context exists:
               └─ createContextImpl(...)
                  └─ ContextRef::create()
                     └─ new M::Context → MAXContext1

KernelLibrary.__init__() makes these calls at graph.py:245:

context = default_mlir_context()
_graph._init_and_register_max_context(context)

The native binding calls getOrCreateContext() at graph.cpp:105.

The exact allocation statement is at Init.cpp:45:

ContextRef ctx = ContextRef::create();

ContextRef is RCRef<M::Context>. Its factory performs new T(...), which here creates M::Context. Sources: Context.h:84, RCRef.h:55.

The initializer then publishes the global context pointer, and the graph binding registers that context with MLIR CTX0:

Global MAX Context pointer ──► MAXContext1
MLIR CTX0 ── registerContext ──► MAXContext1

Sources: Init.cpp:135, graph.cpp:110.

If a MAX Context already exists, getOrCreateContext() checks its initialization options and returns a reference to it. Thus this G1 path can also reuse an existing context. Source: Init.cpp:116.

QnA 2 · What is the difference between MAXContext and MLIR_Ctx0?

MLIR_Ctx0 provides the compiler’s IR environment. MAXContext1 provides shared native services used by the compiler and the engine. They are separate objects connected by a reference. Sources: MLIR’s context definition, and MAX’s Context.h. (mlir.llvm.org)

Aspect MLIR_Ctx0 MAXContext1
Native type mlir::MLIRContext M::Context
Main responsibility Provide the environment for creating and transforming compiler IR Provide access to shared native services
State it holds Dialects, unique type/attribute storage, diagnostics and threading support CPU runtime reference and a typed store of settings, initialization options, telemetry and other services
Example in this walkthrough The environment used when creating G1’s tensor types and mo.graph operations The container through which compiler passes and S1 obtain CPUDevice1

MLIR’s types and attributes are associated with an MLIR context. MAX’s native context stores its CPU runtime separately from its other typed services. Sources: MAX context fields, MAX initialization. (mlir.llvm.org)

For your fused-attention example, the relationship is schematically:

G1._module → mo.graph → B0
└─ IR uses MLIR_Ctx0

MLIR_Ctx0 : mlir::MLIRContext
├─ dialects used by the graph
├─ tensor types and attributes
├─ diagnostics / compiler threading
└─ M dialect
   └─ internal ContextRef ───────────► MAXContext1

MAXContext1 : M::Context
├─ cpuDeviceRef ──► CPUDevice1 ──► work queue Q1
└─ typed service storage
   ├─ M::Config
   ├─ Init::Options
   └─ TelemetryContext

The graph/module/block provide the IR structure. MLIR_Ctx0 supplies the shared context in which its types, attributes and operations are created. MAXContext1 supplies native services accessible from that compiler environment. Sources: graph construction, context association. (mlir.llvm.org)

They are created at different points.

_DEFAULT_MLIR_CONTEXT = mlir.Context()

This creates the default MLIR context during module initialization. Its “current context” selection is thread-local. Source: _mlir_context.py:26.

M::Init::getOrCreateContext("max-graph", options)

G1’s KernelLibrary calls this to create or reuse the native MAX context, then registers it with the MLIR context. Source: graph.cpp:105.

The bridge allows compiler code to reach MAX services:

Compiler pass has MLIR_Ctx0
└─ M::loadContext(MLIR_Ctx0)
   └─ obtain the M dialect’s stored ContextRef
      └─ MAXContext1
         └─ get<AsyncRT::CPUDevice>()
            └─ CPUDevice1

registerContext() installs a dialect extension that stores a copied MAX context reference; loadContext() retrieves it. Sources: registration and lookup, compiler use.

MAXContext1 therefore participates during compilation too. Later, S1’s EngineContext1 retains a reference to that shared MAX context and obtains its CPU runtime. Source: EngineContext.cpp:189.

QnA 3 · What is MAXContext, what does it hold, and how are its services used?

MAXContext1 is a reference-counted M::Context that keeps shared native services together. G1's KernelLibrary creates or reuses it and associates it with MLIR CTX0. The two actual storage members are a CPU runtime reference and a store of objects indexed by their C++ type. Sources: Context.h:29, Context.h:79.

1. The actual storage fields

This is the storage-member excerpt from Context.h:

private:
  GenericRCRef cpuDeviceRef;
  GenericUniquePtrSet storage;

cpuDeviceRef retains an AsyncRT::CPUDevice through a generic reference-counted handle. The generic handle avoids a circular dependency between the context, AsyncRT and the compiler dialects. storage owns the other service objects. Source: setRuntime and its dependency note.

2. What the fresh G1 initialization path installs

When createContextImpl("max-graph", options, "") creates a fresh MAX context, the inventory becomes:

MAXContext1 : M::Context
├─ cpuDeviceRef ──► CPUDevice1 : AsyncRT::CPUDevice
│  └─ workQueue ──► Q1
└─ storage : GenericUniquePtrSet
   ├─ Telemetry::TelemetryContext
   │  └─ constructed from settings, programName="max-graph", subCommand=""
   ├─ M::Config
   │  └─ settings returned by Config::open()
   └─ Init::Options
      ├─ forceDisableCrashReporting = true
      └─ cpuDeviceOptions = present
         ├─ numThreads = 0
         ├─ maxThreads = 0
         └─ mainWillDonate = false

"max-graph" is supplied to the telemetry constructor. The initializer stores the settings and initialization options as service objects. Its CPU-runtime call is getOrCreateCPUDevice(...), so CPUDevice1 and Q1 may already exist even when MAXContext1 is newly allocated. Sources: fresh initialization, telemetry, CPU runtime, stored settings and options.

The displayed options come from G1's native graph binding and the CPUDeviceOptions defaults. The two zero values are requested thread settings; the actual queue's parallelism is determined by runtime setup. The contents of M::Config depend on the settings read in this process. Sources: graph binding options, thread-setting defaults, Init::Options fields.

Held object How it helps
CPUDevice1 Provides the work queue used to schedule native CPU tasks.
M::Config Makes the loaded settings available to native components.
Init::Options Records the options against which a later getOrCreateContext() request is checked.
Telemetry::TelemetryContext Provides telemetry instruments and logging support configured from those settings.

Sources: CPUDevice work queue, reuse checks, telemetry interface.

3. How the typed store works

GenericUniquePtrSet holds at most one object for each concrete C++ type. Its map key is the dense index of TypeID::get<T>(); the value owns that type's object through a GenericUniquePtr. Sources: one object per type, map fields.

storage
├─ TypeID<Telemetry::TelemetryContext> ──► owned telemetry object
├─ TypeID<M::Config>                  ──► owned settings object
└─ TypeID<Init::Options>              ──► owned initialization-options object

The public methods provide these operations:

Call on MAXContext1 Effect
set<T>(unique_ptr) Transfers ownership of an object into the typed store.
emplace<T>(args...) Constructs and stores an object of type T; returns a reference to it.
emplaceIfMissing<T>(args...) Returns the existing object, or constructs it when absent.
createIfMissing<T>(creator) Uses a creator when the service is absent; propagates a creation error.
get<T>() Returns a pointer to the existing object, or nullptr when absent.

Source: Context service methods.

The store locks its map operations. Access to an individual service's mutable state follows that service's own synchronization rules. Source: GenericUniquePtrSet thread-safety contract.

4. How a compiler pass gets a service

For a pass working on G1's IR:

G1's operation → getContext() → MLIR CTX0
└─ M::loadContext(CTX0)
   └─ getOrLoadDialect(M dialect) → getInternal()
      └─ ContextRef ──► MAXContext1
         └─ get<AsyncRT::CPUDevice>()
            ├─ check cpuDeviceRef's TypeID
            └─ matching CPUDevice pointer ──► CPUDevice1
               └─ getWorkQueue() ──► Q1

get<T>() checks the separate CPU-runtime reference first, then checks storage. Looking up M::Config reaches the settings object in the typed store. Looking up an absent service returns nullptr; get<T>() itself does not construct that service. Sources: M::loadContext, Context::get, compiler lookup example.

5. Services that other setup can add

The core initializer above does not enumerate every service that can eventually be present. Other components use the same typed store:

Setup path Service installed or reused Relevant state
M dialect registration, when its pool-installation branch runs AsyncRT::LLVMThreadPool Wraps CPUDevice1; its enqueue path submits tasks to that device's work queue.
S1's Engine::Context::create(...) GML::MaxEngineTelemetryContext Engine telemetry; S1 sets its API language to "python".
A session/C API call that sets a Mojo define KGEN::CompilationContext mojoDefines maps names to bool, int or string values used during JIT compilation.

Sources: conditional thread-pool installation, LLVMThreadPool enqueue, engine telemetry setup, Python API-language input, Mojo-define setter, CompilationContext fields.

The LLVM pool is installed only when registration requests it and MLIR threading is not already enabled. The Mojo-define setter is a possible later use of the store; its presence alone does not imply that this fused-attention caller supplied a Mojo define. Sources: pool branch, define creation.

6. Where the nearby objects live

G1
├─ _module ──► computation IR
└─ _kernel_library
   └─ _analysis ──► Analysis A1
      ├─ context ──► Python MLIR CTX0
      └─ libraryPathsSet ──► P1 paths after import

MLIR CTX0
└─ M dialect's ContextRef ──► MAXContext1
                            ├─ cpuDeviceRef ──► CPUDevice1 / Q1
                            └─ storage ──► shared native services

S1 / NS1
└─ EngineContext1
   ├─ maxContext ──► the same MAXContext1
   └─ config.devices
      ├─ gpu:0 → DC1
      └─ cpu:0 → DCPU1

A1.library_paths = [] describes the declaration-analysis object before import. G1's computation IR, the declaration analysis, MAX's shared service container and the per-device execution contexts have the ownership locations shown above. Sources: Analysis retains its MLIR context and paths, engine context retains MAXContext and config, configured device fields.

Copies of ContextRef retain the same native context. This lets the M dialect and EngineContext1 use the same service objects while each holds its own reference. Source: RCRef::copy.

QnA 4 · What are thread, allocator and pool settings in MAXContext?

Here, thread/allocator/pool settings means AsyncRT::CPUDeviceOptions: the configuration used to create the CPU runtime shared through MAXContext.

The settings and the resulting objects live in different places:

MAXContext1 : M::Context
├─ storage
│  └─ Init::Options
│     └─ cpuDeviceOptions
│        ├─ thread settings
│        ├─ allocator settings
│        └─ work-queue/pool settings
└─ cpuDeviceRef → CPUDevice1
   ├─ allocator → native CPU memory allocator
   └─ workQueue → Q1

MAXContext retains the initialization options and a reference to CPUDevice1. CPUDevice1 owns the actual allocator and work queue. Sources: initialization, CPUDevice members.

Thread settings control how many CPU workers participate. For G1’s initial request:

Setting Value Meaning
numThreads 0 Select the thread count automatically.
maxThreads 0 Use the implementation’s default upper limit.
mainWillDonate false The calling Python thread does not occupy a worker slot in this pool.

Automatic selection considers CPU topology, affinity and process CPU limits. Sources: G1’s options, thread selection.

An illustrative fresh-runtime configuration is:

opts.numThreads = 4;
opts.maxThreads = 4;
opts.mainWillDonate = false;

With sufficient CPU allowance, the pool can have four worker threads handling independent native tasks concurrently—for example, separate compilation jobs. Thread limits can reduce that count. Source: pool construction.

Allocator settings control how CPUDevice1 obtains and releases CPU memory. G1 inherits these defaults:

tcmallocAllocator     = true
leakCheckedAllocator = false
profilingAllocator   = false
useAfterFreeAllocator = false

The creation path selects a base allocator—TCMalloc, ordinary malloc, or the supported use-after-free checker—and can wrap it with leak checking or allocation profiling. Sources: defaults, allocator selection.

For example, a native component could request 64 bytes aligned to 16 bytes:

void *p = allocator->allocateBytes(64, 16);
// use the CPU memory
allocator->deallocateBytes(p, 64);

Those calls go through the selected allocator. Source: allocator interface.

Pool settings choose the task-scheduling mechanism and worker behavior. Relevant G1 defaults are:

Setting Default Purpose
workQueueType kThreadPool Use a queue serviced by worker threads.
threadBusyWaitTime 200 µs Budget for looking for work before idle waiting; an environment variable can override it.
poolName "🔥 Thread" Name used for pool workers.
numaPartitioned false Use the ordinary queue; the partitioned path creates queues for NUMA nodes.

Sources: pool defaults, queue creation.

For example, kThreadPool permits independent tasks to run concurrently, whereas kSingleThread selects a queue that uses one thread. The engine initialization path selects that latter queue when its requested thread count is 1. Source: engine option preparation.

These are CPU runtime resources used by native work. MLIR can also submit compiler tasks to Q1 through its LLVMThreadPool adapter. Source: adapter enqueue path.

CURRENT_GRAPH: ContextVar[Graph] = ContextVar("CURRENT_GRAPH")
# Stack of active Graph.profile_scope() scopes, outermost first. Each entry is
# a (name, color) tuple; color is None when unset. A tuple (not a list) so
# entering/exiting a scope is a ContextVar set()/reset() rather than a
# mutation shared across contexts.
# NOTE: this is a single module-level ContextVar, not scoped per Graph.
# Building a nested Graph while a sibling graph's profile_scope is active will
# inherit that scope's label; this is not currently supported and should be
# avoided.
_CURRENT_PROFILE_SCOPES: ContextVar[tuple[tuple[str, str | None], ...]] = (
    ContextVar("_CURRENT_PROFILE_SCOPES", default=())
)
